Area Siegel–Veech constants for affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero Thanks: Research of D.C. is partially supported by National Science Foundation Grants DMS-2301030, DMS-2001040, Simons Travel Support for Mathematicians, and Simons Fellowship. Thanks: Research of M.M. is partially supported by the DFG-project MO 1884/3-1 and the Collaborative Research Centre TRR 326 “Geometry and Arithmetic of Uniformized Structures.”
Dawei Chen, Elise Goujard, Martin Möller

Abstract
. We describe the principal boundary of an arbitrary affine invariant submanifold of REL zero in terms of level graphs of the multi-scale compactification of strata of Abelian differentials with prescribed orders of zeros. We show that the area Siegel–Veech constant of the affine invariant submanifold can be obtained by using volumes of the principal boundary strata. As an application, we prove the conjectural formula in [ CMS23 ] that computes the area Siegel–Veech constant via intersection theory in the case of REL zero. In particular, the formula holds for strata of quadratic differentials with odd orders of zeros and for the gothic locus. We also explicitly describe the principal boundary components of the gothic locus and their individual contributions to the area Siegel–Veech constant.



[name=graph,title=Graphs and levels,columns=1,columnseprule,options=-s Idx.ist]

1.
Introduction

For a partition
\mu=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
of
2g-2
, i.e.,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}=2g-2
, let
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
be the stratum of Abelian differentials
\omega
on smooth and connected complex curves
X
of genus
g
that have labeled zeros whose orders are specified by
\mu
. Such differentials correspond to flat (or translation) surfaces with conical singularities (or saddle points) at the zeros, where the cone angle at each zero is
(m_{i}+1)2\pi
. The study of differentials and flat surfaces is important for surface dynamics and moduli theory. In particular, there is a
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-action on
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
by affine transformations, which is called Teichmüller dynamics. The
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-orbit closures have a locally linear structure under the period coordinates of the ambient stratum (see
[
EM18
,
EMM15
]
), and hence they are also called
affine invariant submanifolds
(or
linear subvarieties
from a more algebraic viewpoint).

A number of intriguing dynamical invariants can be defined for the strata, and more generally, for affine invariant submanifolds. Among them, Siegel–Veech constants indicate the asymptotic growth rates of cylinders or saddle connections with bounded lengths for a generic flat surface parameterized in an affine invariant submanifold. Relatedly, Lyapunov exponents measure the separation rates of infinitesimally closed trajectories under the Teichmüller geometric flow. Note that the sum of Lyapunov exponents and the
area
Siegel–Veech constant (i.e., counting cylinders weighted by their areas) determine each other (see
[
EKZ14
]
).

Our aim here is to compute the area Siegel–Veech constants through intersection theory on compactified affine invariant submanifolds. We set up some notation first.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an affine invariant submanifold in a stratum of Abelian differentials
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
. Suppose the tangent space of
\mathcal{M}
projects onto a subspace
A
of absolute periods, with kernel
R
of relative periods. Let
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}A=a
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}R=r
, so that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}=a+r=:m+1
, where
m
is the projectivized dimension of
\mathcal{M}
.

Denote by
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{M}
the projectivization of
\mathcal{M}
and by

\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}
(the normalization of) the closure of
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{M}
in the
multi-scale compactification of the projectivized stratum of Abelian differentials. Then
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}=m=(a-1)+r
. Let
\xi

be the first Chern class of the universal line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)
on
\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}
,
\delta

the boundary divisor class, and
\psi_{i}
the cotangent line class associated to the
i
-th zero.
In
[
CMS23
, Conjecture 4.3]
, the following conjectural formula was proposed for computing the area Siegel–Veech constant

c_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{M})
via intersection theory:

(1)

\displaystyle c_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\;=\;}}-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{a-2}\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{r}\delta}{\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{a-1}\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{r}}\,.

In this paper, we prove the conjectural formula in the case of REL zero, i.e., when
r=0
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an affine invariant submanifold of REL zero and complex dimension
m+1
. Then the area Siegel–Veech constant of
\mathcal{M}
is equal to

(2)

\displaystyle c_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{M})\;=\;-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m-1}\delta}{\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m}}\,.

The sum of (nonnegative) Lyapunov exponents of
\mathcal{M}
is determined by
c_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{M})
via
[
EKZ14
, Formula (2.1)]
:

L(\mathcal{M})\;=\;\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}c_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{M})+\frac{1}{12}\kappa_{\mu}

where
\kappa_{\mu}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}(m_{i}+2)/(m_{i}+1)
for
\mu=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
. The algebro-geometric counterpart of this formula is the relation of divisor classes
\lambda=(\delta+\kappa)/12

on the moduli space of curves, where
\lambda
is the first Chern class of the Hodge bundle and

\kappa
is the first Miller–Morita–Mumford class. Moreover, the relation of divisor classes

\kappa=-\kappa_{\mu}\xi

holds on the multi-scale compactification of the strata of differentials modulo the boundary divisors
\delta_{\Lambda}
with two-level graphs
\Lambda
(see
[
CCM24
, Proposition 6.3]
). Since

\xi^{m-1}\delta_{\Lambda}=0
in the case of REL zero (see Lemma
5.1
), combining these relations with Formula (
2
) thus leads to a companion formula for computing the sum of Lyapunov exponents of
\mathcal{M}
via intersection theory.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an affine invariant submanifold of REL zero and complex dimension
m+1
. Then the sum of Lyapunov exponents of
\mathcal{M}
is equal to

(3)

\displaystyle L(\mathcal{M})\;=\;-\frac{\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m-1}\lambda}{\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m}}\,.

The lowest dimensional affine invariant submanifolds are Teichmüller curves, which are of REL zero,
whose (unprojectivized) dimension is two, and whose corresponding
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-orbits are closed in
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
. Prominent examples of higher-dimensional affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero include the minimal strata of Abelian differentials
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}(2g-2)
, strata of quadratic differentials with odd orders of zeros only, and the gothic locus
[
MMW17
]
. Formula (
2
) was previously known to hold for Teichmüller curves
[
Che11
,
CM12
]
, for the minimal strata
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}(2g-2)

[
Sau18
,
CMSZ20
]
, and for the principal strata of quadratic differentials with simple zeros
[
CMS23
]
. Concerning the gothic locus, the intersection numbers on the right-hand side of (
2
) were evaluated in
[
Che22
]
(without proving the correspondence to the area Siegel–Veech constant). Therefore, Theorem
1.1
implies the following new advances in this direction.

Corollary 1.3
.

Formula (
2
) holds for all strata of quadratic differentials with only odd orders of zeros and for the gothic locus. In particular, the area Siegel–Veech constant
of the gothic locus is
49/(13\pi^{2})
. Moreover, the gothic locus has four types of principal boundary graphs and their individual contributions to the area Siegel–Veech constant can be determined explicitly (see Proposition
6.2
).

Now we explain the strategy of proving Theorem
1.1
. First, we describe a set of level graphs (in the sense of
[
BCGGM19
]
) such that all non-zero contributions to Siegel–Veech constants are supported on a neighborhood of these graphs. We call these graphs
principal graphs
, as they generalize the role played by the principal boundary in the sense of
[
EMZ03
]
for Abelian differentials (see also

[
Gou15
]
for the case of quadratic differentials). Note that in our setting, principal graphs do not correspond to boundary divisors of the multi-scale compactification except for the case of Teichmüller curves, but to higher-codimensional boundary strata. The reason why they are nevertheless principal for volume computations is explained in Section
3.4
. Next, we derive an expression for
c_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{M})
in terms of volumes of the top level strata of principal graphs using flat surface geometry. Then we show that the right-hand side of Formula (
2
) can also be reduced to the principal graphs via divisor relations on the multi-scale compactification of affine invariant submanifolds. Finally, we show that the resulting two expressions coincide with each other.

The assumption of REL zero plays an essential role for defining a natural volume form on
\mathcal{M}
, by using the area form of flat surfaces and the associated Hermitian metric on the
\mathcal{O}(-1)
bundle. If
\mathcal{M}
has nonzero REL, then the part of relative periods is not captured in the area form, and hence it is unclear how to canonically define a volume form on
\mathcal{M}
. Nevertheless, once we can make sense of substituting relative periods by using
\psi
-classes to obtain the volume of
\mathcal{M}
via intersection theory, then we expect that the strategy in this paper can be adapted to prove (
1
) in full generality. On the other hand, as we have seen, in the case of REL zero the intersection-theoretic formula can already help us evaluate area Siegel–Veech constants, e.g., for the gothic locus. There are several other newly discovered affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero in
[
EMMW20
]
. It would be interesting to analyze their principal graphs and then apply our result to evaluate their area Siegel–Veech constants. We plan to treat these questions in future work.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we review basic properties of affine invariant submanifolds and their closures in the multi-scale compactification of strata of differentials. In Section
3
, we define and study the principal boundary components as well as their associated graphs for Siegel–Veech constants. In Section
4
, we reduce the computation for the area and cylinder Siegel–Veech constants of an affine invariant submanifold
\mathcal{M}
of REL zero to weighted sums of volumes of the principal boundary components mod out the volume of
\mathcal{M}
. In Section
5
, we show that the expression of the volume reduction obtained in Section
4
for the area Siegel–Veech constant coincides with that from the intersection calculation, thus proving Theorem
1.1
. Finally, in Section
6
, we provide a number of known examples of affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero. In particular, we describe the principal boundary components of the gothic locus and determine their respective contributions to the area Siegel–Veech constant.

2.
Affine invariant submanifolds and their closures

2.1.
Affine invariant submanifolds

Let
\mu=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
be a tuple of nonnegative integers with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}=2g-2
. Denote by
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
the moduli space of Abelian differentials
(X,\omega)
of type
\mu
with the
n
zeros
Z(\omega)
of the differential
\omega
labeled as marked points in the underlying surface
X
. By the fundamental results of
[
EM18
,
EMM15
]
, the closure of any
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-orbit in
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
locally consists of a finite union of linear subspaces in period coordinate charts. For this reason, we define an
affine invariant submanifold

\mathcal{M}
of
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
as an algebraic stack with a map
\mathcal{M}\to{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
which is the
normalization
of a
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-orbit closure as its image. We write
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{M}
for the projectivization of
\mathcal{M}
modulo scaling of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

We recall a few fundamental properties of affine invariant submanifolds
(see
[
Wri14
]
). The projection

p\colon H_{1}(X,Z(\omega),\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(X,\mathbb{R})

from relative homology to absolute homology induces a short exact sequence

0\to\ker(p)\cap T\mathcal{M}\to T\mathcal{M}\to p(T\mathcal{M})\to 0

where
T\mathcal{M}
is the tangent bundle to
\mathcal{M}
. The image
p(T\mathcal{M})
is of
even

dimension

a\;=\;2\rho\,.

We refer to
\rho
as the
rank
of
\mathcal{M}

and denote by
r=\dim(\ker(p)\cap T\mathcal{M})
the
rank of relative periods

of
\mathcal{M}
. If
r=0
, we say that
\mathcal{M}
has
REL zero
. The tangent bundle
T\mathcal{M}

is defined over a number field. There is a minimal such number field, called
the
affine field of definition
k(\mathcal{M})
.

The differential
\omega
induces a flat metric on
X
with conical singularities at the zeros of
\omega
. For this reason,
(X,\omega)
is also called a
flat surface
(or a
translation surface
). Following
[
Wri15
]
we say that a collection
C_{1},\ldots,C_{t}
of cylinders on a flat surface
(X,\omega)
in
\mathcal{M}
is
\mathcal{M}
-parallel
, if the core curves of the cylinders are collinear and stay collinear
in a neighborhood of the point parameterizing
(X,\omega)
in
\mathcal{M}
.

2.2.
The multi-scale compactification

Let
\mathbb{P}\Xi\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
be the moduli space of projectivized multi-scale differentials constructed in
[
BCGGM19
]
, which compactifies the moduli space
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
. In what follows we recall some properties about the boundary of
\mathbb{P}\Xi\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
.

The boundary strata of
\mathbb{P}\Xi\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
are indexed by level graphs. A
level
graph

\Gamma
consists of a stable graph (of the underlying pointed stable curve) together with a weak total order on the vertices, usually given by a
level function normalized to have top level zero, and an enhancement
\kappa_{e}\geq 0
associated to each edge
e
. An edge is called
horizontal
if
it starts and ends at the same level, and called
vertical
if it joins two vertices at different levels. Moreover,
\kappa_{e}=0
if and only if the edge
e
is horizontal. We let

\ell_{i}\;=\;\operatorname{lcm}(\kappa_{e}:\text{$e$ crosses the passage above level\penalty\ $i$})\,.

The union of vertices at any given level of a level graph defines
the type of a
generalized stratum
, that is a stratum of possibly
meromorphic differentials on a possibly disconnected complex curve, which is moreover
constrained by residue conditions. Here the components of the curve
are given by the vertices of
\Gamma
at the given level, and the orders
of zeros and poles of the differential are determined by the half-edge labels
and the enhancements. We refer to
[
CMZ18
, Section 4]
for more
details and for a way to encode the residue conditions. A collection
of differentials in the generalized strata for each level gives a

twisted differential
compatible with
\Gamma
. A
multi-scale
differential
(compatible with
\Gamma
) is
a twisted differential together with another discrete datum, a collection
of
prong-matchings
, and with the differentials on
lower level defined up to projectivization. Given a level graph
\Gamma
, we
denote by
D_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
the boundary stratum of all multi-scale
differentials compatible with
\Gamma
and we denote by
D_{\Gamma}
its closure.

Fix a level graph
\Gamma
with
L
levels below zero. Then the boundary
divisor decomposes up to commensurability into products of generalized strata
of differentials. Here the generalized strata
B_{\Gamma}^{[i]}
allow for
disconnected curves and the differentials may be constrained by residue
conditions. Commensurability means the existence of the following diagram
(see
[
CMS23a
, Diagram (5)]
)

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Gamma}^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=-L}^{0}B_{\Gamma}^{[i]}=:{B}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\Gamma}}

where
p_{\Gamma}
and
c_{\Gamma}
are finite maps when restricted to the
preimage of
D_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
, starting at some space
D_{\Gamma}^{s}
(referring to a “simple” twist group) whose precise definition is not needed here.

The generalized strata
B_{\Gamma}^{[i]}
at each level are smooth stacks of some dimension
N_{0}
resp. of dimension
N_{i}-1
for
i=-1,\ldots,-L
(duo to level-wise projectivization below the top level). By construction we have the relation

(5)

\sum_{i=0}^{-L}N_{i}\;=\;N\coloneqq\dim\Xi\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)

among the (unprojectivized) dimensions of the level strata and the ambient
moduli space. For graphs with only two levels we often write

N^{\top}\coloneqq N_{0}
and
N^{\bot}\coloneqq N_{-1}
.

2.3.
The closures of affine invariant submanifolds

Given an affine invariant submanifold
\mathcal{M}
, we denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
the normalization of the closure of the image of
\mathcal{M}
as a substack of
\Xi\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
. The shape of local equations cutting out
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
in
\Xi\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
has been determined in
[
BDG22
]
. We summarize their results and adapt to our case of affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero.

Local defining equations of
\mathcal{M}
can be interpreted as homology classes. We say that a horizontal node is
crossed
by an equation, if the corresponding
vanishing cycle has non-trivial intersection with the homology class of the equation.
The horizontal nodes are partitioned into
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence
classes
by simultaneous appearance in equations of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e., a collection
of nodes belongs to the same
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence class, defined as follows. If there is an equation crossing a set of nodes, but no equation crosses a proper subset of them, then the nodes are called
\mathcal{M}
-cross-related
. The equivalence relation generated by this relation is
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence.
A starting observation
[
BDG22
, Theorem 1.1]
is that
\omega
-periods of
the vanishing cycles in an
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence class are proportional.
In particular, horizontal nodes in the same
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence class
are on the same level.

Given a level graph
\Gamma
with
L
levels below zero, we briefly recall the coordinates near the corresponding boundary stratum
D_{\Gamma}
. They consist first of
level coordinates

t_{i}
for
i=-1,\ldots,-L
that record the scaling of the
i
-th level. Second, for each horizontal edge at level
i
, there is a coordinate
q^{[i]}_{j}
that records the opening up of the corresponding node. Third, the remaining coordinates are period coordinates of differentials at the various levels. (At this stage we are glossing over the details where the latter are not always well-defined. To remedy this, one uses perturbed period coordinates
[
BCGGM19
,
CMZ22
]
, or log period coordinates
[
BDG22
]
. However we will not need those details in this paper.)

In
[
BDG22
]
, it shows that the equations cutting out the image of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}

in
\Xi\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
can be generated by the following two types of equations.
First, there are
level-wise equations
among the period coordinates.
They are linear and involve only coordinates at one level. Second, there
are
horizontal crossing equations
. These are multiplicative
equations among the
q^{[i]}_{j}
for a given level
i
. In particular, there
are no relations among the
t_{i}
. For the case of REL zero, we can make the
equations involving the
q^{[i]}_{j}
and the vanishing cycles even more
precise as follows.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
BDG22
, Theorem 1.10]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an affine invariant submanifold of REL zero. Then the space of horizontal crossing equations of the image of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
is generated by equations
of the form
(q^{[i]}_{j_{1}})^{a}=(q^{[i]}_{j_{2}})^{b}
. Moreover, the periods of the vanishing cycles of the corresponding nodes are proportional. In particular, the normalization
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
is smooth.

In this theorem the exponents
a
and
b
are a priori real numbers just quoting

[
BDG22
]
. We will show in Lemma
3.6
that they are in
fact integers.

Proof.

The corresponding result for
\mathcal{M}
in the minimal stratum
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}(2g-2)
(as a standard example of REL zero) was stated in
[
BDG22
, Theorem 1.10]
. Indeed the proof only relied on the assumption of REL zero (i.e., the maps
\iota
and
u
are isomorphisms in
[
BDG22
, Section 6.1]
). Therefore, the same proof works for all affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero.
∎

As a consequence of the structure of equations and the commensurability
diagram (
4
), we obtain a similar commensurability
decomposition of the boundary stratum
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
, defined as the
intersection of
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}
with the preimage of
D_{\Gamma}
, into generalized
linear submanifolds
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{[i]}
. Restricted to the open
set
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
over
D_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
, we have the following diagram

(6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=-L}^{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{[i]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}\,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\Gamma,{\mathcal{M}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\Gamma,{\mathcal{M}}}}

for some space
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{s}
(see
[
CMS23a
, Diagram (13)]
). In the case of two levels, i.e.,
L=1
, we write
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{\top}:={\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{[0]}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{\bot}:={\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{[-1]}
for the two levels of the decomposition up to commensurability. In this case,
\ell=\ell_{1}=\operatorname{lcm}(\kappa_{e})
, where
e\in E(\Gamma)
runs over all (vertical) edges. We let
N_{\mathcal{M}}^{\top}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{\top}
and
N_{\mathcal{M}}^{\bot}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{\bot}+1
be the dimensions of the unprojectivized level strata.
These dimensions are related by

(7)

N_{\mathcal{M}}^{\top}+N_{\mathcal{M}}^{\bot}\;=\;m+1\,

where
m
is the projectivized dimension of
{\mathcal{M}}
.

If we moreover restrict to a component of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
(we do this tacitly here and will introduce notation for them along with additional data in Section
3.5
), we find specifically (see
[
CMS23a
, Lemma 3.6]
) that

(8)

\deg(c_{\Gamma})\;=\;\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Gamma)\,,\qquad\deg(p_{\Gamma})\;=\;\frac{K^{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}{\ell_{\Gamma}}\,.

Here
\operatorname{Aut}_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Gamma)
is the subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)
whose induced
action on a neighborhood of
D_{\Gamma}
preserves the image of
{\mathcal{M}}
in the
ambient stratum of differentials. Moreover,
K_{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{M}}
is the number of reachable
prong-matchings with respect to
\mathcal{M}
, which is described in

[
CMS23a
, Theorem 1.3]
.

3.
Siegel–Veech constants and principal graphs

3.1.
Siegel–Veech constants

Siegel–Veech constants measure the growth rates of the numbers of saddle connections or cylinders (with bounded lengths or widths) on a flat surface, possibly with additional constraints on the position of zeros relative to the saddle connections or cylinders. More precisely, these quantities all exhibit a quadratic growth rate and the Siegel–Veech constant is the leading term of large length asymptotics. Siegel–Veech constants can alternatively be defined as the comparison coefficient between two integrals. We will take this as our definition (see (
10
) below and refer to
[
EM01
,
EMZ03
,
CMZ18
]
for the conversion of this coefficient into growth rate asymptotics).

We start by recalling the notion of Siegel–Veech transform.
Let
V=V(X,\omega)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a function that associates with a flat
surface a subset in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with (real) multiplicities, satisfying the
Siegel–Veech axioms (see Section 2 in
[
EM01
]
). These axioms
are roughly the
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-equivariance, the quadratic growth rate of
V
,
and an integrability condition (see the examples below). For any function

\chi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
we denote by
\widehat{\chi}
the Siegel–Veech transform
with respect to
V
as follows:

(9)

\widehat{\chi}(X,\omega)\;=\;\sum_{v\in V(X,\omega)}\chi(v)\,.

Let
\nu
be a finite
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant measure on a subset of the
hypersurface
{\Omega_{1}\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\mu)
of area-one flat surfaces,
whose support we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{1}
. By the fundamental results of

[
EM18
]
and
[
EMM15
]
, this support is actually the area-one
hypersurface in an affine invariant submanifold. The results of Veech and Eskin–Masur
(
[
Vee98
]
,
[
EM01
]
) jointly imply that for appropriate

V
and
\nu
there is a
Siegel–Veech constant

c(\nu,V)
, such that
for all functions
\chi
we have

(10)

\frac{1}{\nu(H)}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}\widehat{\chi}d\nu\;=\;c(\nu,V)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi dxdy\,.

Note that rescaling
\nu
by a scalar factor leaves the Siegel–Veech constant
c(\nu,V)

unchanged.

In this paper we consider the following types of Siegel–Veech constants. If
V
is the vector function of all core curves of cylinders with weight one, we obtain the
cylinder Siegel–Veech constant

c_{\operatorname{cyl}}
. Taking again the core curves of cylinders, but with weight given by the area of the cylinder, we obtain the
area Siegel–Veech constant

c_{\operatorname{area}}
.

3.2.
Volume normalization

By
[
EM18
]
and
[
EMM15
]
, every
\mathbb{R}
-linear submanifold
\mathcal{M}
comes
with a unique (up to scale)
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant ergodic volume form, whose
restriction to the (real) hypersurface
\mathcal{M}_{1}
of area-one flat surfaces
is the
\nu
introduced above. Our
goal here is to fix a scale
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}
for the measure of all these
linear submanifolds, derived from the mere existence of the area form
on the flat surface. The following is a summary of
[
Ngu25
, Section 6.1]
for this purpose, for which we also assume
REL zero in this subsection
.

Recall that
\mathcal{M}
has complex dimension
m+1
. There are two ways to define
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}
. By
[
AEM17
]
the restriction of the area form
h
induced by the flat metric to the tangent space
T\mathcal{M}
is a symplectic form. Consequently,
taking the imaginary part
\vartheta
of this symplectic form, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\bf i}\vartheta)}^{m+1}/(m+1)!
is a volume form. In a local chart where
\mathcal{M}
is modeled on the vector space
V
, the image under period coordinates is actually in the orthant
V^{+}
where
h
is positive. There we can write

(11)

\vartheta\;=\;\frac{{{\bf i}}}{2}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{(m+1)/2}d{A_{i}}\wedge d\bar{A_{i}}-d{B_{i}}\wedge d\bar{B_{i}}\Big)\,,

where
a_{i}=\int_{\alpha_{i}}\omega
and
b_{i}=\int_{\beta_{i}}\omega
are
local coordinates obtained as periods of a
symplectic

basis
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
of the (real) tangent space
T_{\mathcal{M}}
, and

(12)

A_{i}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}(a_{i}-{\bf i}b_{i})\,,\quad B_{i}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}(a_{i}+{\bf i}b_{i})\,.

This defines the auxiliary
intersection volume form

(13)

\nu_{\rm int}=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\bf i}\vartheta)}^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}

on
V
. For a measurable subset
B\subset\mathcal{M}_{1}

that fits in one period coordinate chart, we denote by
C(B)
the cone in
V^{+}
bounded
by
B
. We define the
natural normalization of the volume form on
\mathcal{M}

to be

(14)

\nu_{\mathcal{M}}(B)=\nu_{\rm int}(C(B))

and extend to general
B
by additivity. Similarly we define a measure
\overline{\nu}
on
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{M}
by

(15)

\overline{\nu}_{\mathcal{M}}(B)=\nu_{\rm int}(\widehat{C}(B))

where the cone
\widehat{C}(B)
is
the intersection of the preimage of
B
in
\mathcal{M}
with the locus
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}
of flat surfaces of area
\leq 1
.

For the second definition, consider the
(1,1)
-form

\varpi=\frac{-1}{2\pi{\bf i}}\partial\bar{\partial}\log(h)\,.

Its top wedge power defines another
volume form. A local computation (
[
Ngu25
, Lemma 6.1]
) shows that

\overline{\nu}_{\mathcal{M}}\;=\;(-1)^{m}\cdot\frac{2\pi}{2^{m+1}}\cdot\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}^{m+1}}}{(m+1)!}\cdot(-2\pi\varpi)^{m}\;=\;\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\bf i}\pi)}^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}\cdot\varpi^{m}\,.

On the other hand, the cohomology class of
\varpi
is precisely
c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))=\xi
(see
[
Sau18
,
CMZ24
]
). We thus obtain that

(16)

\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})\,:=\,(2m+2)\overline{\nu}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbb{P}\mathcal{M})\;=\;(2m+2)\nu_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathcal{M}_{1})\;=\;\frac{2}{m!}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}}\pi)^{m+1}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m}\,.

Finally, it is sometimes convenient to define a volume element

\nu_{\rm dis}
on the hypersurface
\mathcal{M}_{1}
of area-one flat surfaces by disintegration, i.e., by
the requirement

(17)

d\nu_{\rm int}=r^{2m+1}drd\nu_{\rm dis}\,

where
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{M}}=2m+2
. These volume forms are related by

(18)

\nu_{\rm dis}(U)\;=\;\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\nu_{{\mathcal{M}}}(U)\,,\qquad\text{hence}\qquad\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})\;=\;\nu_{\rm dis}({\mathcal{M}}_{1})\,

(see
[
EMZ03
, Section 2.2]
).

We remark that when
\mathcal{M}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, e.g., for strata of differentials and the gothic locus, one can use alternative volume normalizations by
choosing a unit cube under period coordinates (see
[
EMZ03
]
,
[
Gou15
]
and
[
TT20
]
). Nevertheless, the volume normalization by using the area form
\varpi
in the above seems more intrinsic, as it can also work when the period coordinates do not span a lattice.

3.3.
Principal graphs

We will show that boundary strata with the following type of level graphs
\Gamma
support all the contributions to cylinder and area Siegel–Veech constants. We remark that the notion below does not only depend on the graph, but also on the top and bottom strata
\mathcal{M}^{\top}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\bot}
that potentially appear in the boundary intersection of
\mathcal{M}
with
D_{\Gamma}
. The main result of this subsection holds for general REL.

Definition 3.1
.

(1)
Suppose the affine invariant submanifold
\mathcal{M}
has REL zero and rank one, i.e., it is
a Teichmüller curve. In this case a level graph
\Gamma
is called a
principal
graph (with respect to
\mathcal{M}
), if it has one level and its horizontal edges
form one
\mathcal{M}
-parallelity class and one
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence class.

(2)
Suppose the affine invariant submanifold
\mathcal{M}
has rank at least two. A level graph
\Gamma
is called a
principal graph (with respect to
\mathcal{M}
)
, if

i)

the level graph
\Gamma
has exactly two levels,

ii)

the top level of
\Gamma
has no horizontal edges,

iii)

the unprojectivized bottom level dimension
N_{\Gamma}^{\bot}=1
, and

iv)

there is exactly one
\mathcal{M}
-parallelity class of horizontal edges on
the lower level.

If
\mathcal{M}
has REL zero, then we moreover require that

(R)

the horizontal edges form one
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence class.

We remark that if
\mathcal{M}
has nonzero REL, then condition (R) has to be modified, and we do not address the details here since our main application in this paper is for the case of REL zero.

Additionally, we do not claim that
D_{\Gamma}
with any such
\Gamma
has nonempty intersection with
\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}
. If this is not the case, the contribution of
D_{\Gamma}
to the
concerned Siegel–Veech constants will be zero, and conversely, we show that the
contribution is non-zero otherwise. Additionally, in the case of rank one, i.e., for Teichmüller curves, there do not exist boundary points with two-level principal graphs for the dimension reason.

In the case of REL zero, the next lemma ensures that the boundary defined by principal graphs stays within the scope of affine invariant submanifolds for which we can give a volume normalization.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose the affine invariant submanifold
\mathcal{M}
has REL zero and
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})
is at least two. If
\Gamma
is a graph satisfying i)–iv) in Definition
3.1
, then the top level
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top}
also has REL zero, and
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top})=\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})-1
. In particular,
\Gamma
satisfies (R) and hence it is a principal graph in the case of REL zero.

Proof.

If
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top})>\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})-1
, then
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top}>2\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})-2
. Since the period around the node
for one of the horizontal edges on lower level as well as the level
coordinate
t_{-1}
contribute additionally to the dimension of
\mathcal{M}
, we
obtain
\dim\mathcal{M}>2\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})
, which leads to a contradiction.

On the other hand, if
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top})<\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})-1
, then there are
at least two symplectic pairs in the tangent space to
\mathcal{M}
that do not degenerate
to absolute periods on top level. Moreover, relative periods on top level only
contribute to a subspace on which the intersection form is trivial. Consequently,
there is a rank-two subspace of
T_{\mathcal{M}}
that degenerates to lower level on
which the intersection form is trivial. A principal graph has

N_{\Gamma}^{\bot}=1
, which is necessarily generated by a curve around the node
for one of the horizontal edges, and the periods created by opening the
horizontal nodes intersect this generator non-trivially. Therefore, there
can be at most one symplectic pair that degenerates to the lower level, which again leads to a contradiction.

Finally, since
\dim\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top}=2\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top})
, it follows that
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top}
has REL zero. If there are more than one
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence classes on lower level, then the codimension of
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\top}
would be bigger than two and hence has rank less than
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})-1
, leading to a contradiction. Therefore,
\Gamma
satisfies (R) and it is a principal graph.
∎

We now prove a preliminary version of our goal.

Proposition 3.3
.

There exist
local Siegel–Veech constants

c_{\star}({\mathcal{M}},\Gamma)\geq 0

such that

(19)

c_{\star}(\mathcal{M})\;=\;\sum_{\Gamma\penalty\ \rm{principal}}c_{\star}({\mathcal{M}},\Gamma)

for
\star\in\{\operatorname{area},\operatorname{cyl}\}
.

Suppose moreover that
{\mathcal{M}}
has REL zero. Then
c_{\star}({\mathcal{M}},\Gamma)>0
if and only if
D^{\circ}_{\Gamma}\cap\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
be the locus in
\mathcal{M}
parameterizing flat surfaces
S

of area one with a cylinder of perimeter
\leq\varepsilon
. As an immediate consequence
of the Siegel–Veech formula (valid for any affine invariant
submanifold), we obtain as in
[
EMZ03
, Proposition 3.3]
that

(20)

c_{\star}(\mathcal{M})\;=\;\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\pi\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Vol}_{\star}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon})}{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})}\,

where
\operatorname{Vol}_{\star}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon})
is defined as the weighted volume

\operatorname{Vol}_{\star}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon})=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{M}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon}}W_{\star}(S)d\nu_{\mathcal{M}}(S)

with the weights
W_{\star}
depending only on the family of
\mathcal{M}
-parallel cylinders
in
S
. Explicit formulas for the weights are given in (
34
)
below.

Generalizing the strategy in
[
EMZ03
]
, we define
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
as the
subset of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
consisting of surfaces with the following properties:

•

There is exactly one family
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{j}\}_{j=1}^{q}
of
\mathcal{M}
-parallel
cylinders having at least one cylinder of perimeter
\leq\varepsilon
. We order the
cylinders increasingly by the length of their waist curves
w(C_{i})
and set

W=w(C_{q})/w(C_{1})
.

•

All saddle connections at the boundary of any of the
C_{j}
are

\mathcal{M}
-parallel.

•

The length of any saddle connection not at the boundary of any of the
C_{j}
is larger than
3W\varepsilon
.

We let
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thin}}
be the complement of the thick part in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.
We claim that
[
Doz23
, Theorem 1.3]
implies that

\operatorname{Vol}_{\ast}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon})\;=\;\operatorname{Vol}_{\ast}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}})+o(\varepsilon^{2})\,.

In fact the weight function is bounded by the number of cylinders, which is
bounded by
3g-3+n
and Dozier has shown that the volume of the subset
parametrizing surfaces with two non-
\mathcal{M}
-parallel saddle connections of lengths

\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
is
O(\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\varepsilon_{2}^{2})
.

Assume from now on that
\mathrm{rank}(\mathcal{M})>1
. We now associate with every surface
(X,\omega)
in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
a principal graph
\Gamma
as follows. Consider the tubular neighborhood
T\subset X
consisting of all points that have distance
\leq\varepsilon
to a point in
\mathcal{C}
. The rank
hypothesis and the last condition of the thick part together ensure that

X\setminus T
is non-empty. The vertices on top level of
\Gamma
correspond to the components of
X\setminus T
, and the vertices on lower level correspond to the components of
T
with the core curves of all the cylinders in
{\mathcal{C}}
removed. The vertical edges of
\Gamma
correspond to the boundary components of the closure of
X\setminus T
, decorated with
\kappa_{e}\geq 0
where
2\pi\kappa_{e}
is the total curvature of the boundary component for
e
. The legs of
\Gamma
are given by the zeros of
\omega
with obvious adjacency to the vertices. Finally, the horizontal edges of
\Gamma
correspond to pinching the core curves of the cylinders in
{\mathcal{C}}
.

To show that
\Gamma
is indeed a principal graph, we remark that all saddle
connections in
T
are
\mathcal{M}
-parallel to the core curve of some
C_{i}
by the
first condition in the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
and thus
N_{\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}}^{\bot}=1
.
The other conditions are obvious by construction, except for the additional
condition (R) in the case of REL zero. If there are more than one
{\mathcal{M}}
-cross-equivalence
classes on lower level, then the codimension of
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}\cap D_{\Gamma}
is bigger than two. Given that the horizontal edges are on lower level, this contradicts Lemma
3.2
.

We call the level graph
\Gamma
the
configuration
associated with

(X,\omega)\in\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
and write
\mathcal{M}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
for the subset
of surfaces with configuration
\Gamma
. With the definition

(21)

c_{\star}(\mathcal{M},\Gamma)\;=\;\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\pi\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Vol}_{\star}(\mathcal{M}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})}{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})}\,,

the non-negativity claim is obvious and the claimed identity (
19
)
follows since we only neglected terms of higher order in
\varepsilon
. The positivity
claim can be completed by using Proposition
4.4
which is stated later.

Finally we deal with the case of
\mathrm{rank}(\mathcal{M})=1
. In this case, the graph
\Gamma
associated with
(X,\omega)\in\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
has vertices corresponding to the components of
X\setminus\mathcal{C}
and a horizontal edge for each cylinder. Note that all the cylinders are
\mathcal{M}
-parallel and their heights are pairwise proportional. Hence, they form one
\mathcal{M}
-cross-equivalence class, which implies that
\Gamma
is indeed a principal graph in this case. The remaining part of the statement is obvious by the well-known degeneration behavior of Teichmüller curves.
∎

The above proof indicates that we should decompose surfaces in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
further within their upper level and lower level in order to compute
c_{\star}({\mathcal{M}},\Gamma)
through the product decomposition. This will be achieved in Proposition
3.8
later.

Depending on the context, sometimes a collection of
{\mathcal{M}}
-parallel cylinders is also called a “configuration” in the literature. In our context, these contain the partial information of the level graph after undegenerating the vertical level passage, retaining the horizontal edges only. This partial information is not sufficient for our aim, as is already visible by the fact that surfaces parametrized by such one-level horizontal boundary components have infinite volume, incompatible with the basic formula (
21
).

3.4.
Principal boundary but higher codimension

The goal of this section is to explain why principal graphs can define
boundary strata of codimension greater than one in the multi-scale compactification and yet give the
principal
boundary for computing Siegel–Veech
constants for affine invariant submanifolds of rank greater than one. This
apparent contradiction can be clarified by considering the area renormalization.

1
1
-2

0

2
1
-2

0

2
b
c
d
b
d
a
c
a

Figure 1.
The level graphs
\Gamma_{1}
(left) and
\Gamma_{2}
(middle) and parameters on a flat surface in
\Omega{\mathcal{M}}_{2}(2)
(right)

We consider the stratum
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{2}(2)
as an example. Let
D_{\Gamma_{1}}
be the boundary stratum of codimension one given by the level graph
\Gamma_{1}
of compact type with a vertex of genus one at each of the two levels, and let
D_{\Gamma_{2}}
be the boundary stratum of codimension two obtained by degenerating the lower level vertex of
\Gamma_{1}
into a horizontal loop (see Figure
1
). The path
\gamma_{1}(\varepsilon)
in
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{2}(2)
consisting of flat surfaces of area one as in this figure with
a=\varepsilon
and
c=\varepsilon
while
b=1
and
d=1-\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}
converges to a point in
D_{\Gamma_{1}}
as
\varepsilon
tends to zero. On the other hand, the path
\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon)
with
a=\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and
c=\varepsilon
while
b=1
and
d=1-\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{3/2}
converges to a point in
D_{\Gamma_{2}}
. (This can be rigorously justified in the quasi-conformal topology.
To construct the maps, say for
\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon)
, cut out a neighborhood of the small top cylinder and cut this cylinder moreover along its core curve. This sequence of subsurfaces rescaled by a factor of
1/\sqrt{\varepsilon}
converges quasi-conformally to a four-punctured sphere on the bottom level, and the rest
of the surface converges to a flat torus on the top level.)

This implies that neither the limit point
\gamma_{1}(0)
of the first path
nor any small neighborhood of it lies in a small tubular neighborhood of
D_{\Gamma_{2}}
.
Nevertheless, any point in the path
\gamma_{1}(\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}

lies in the
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{0}/(1-\varepsilon_{0}^{2})
-neighborhood
\mathcal{M}_{1,\Gamma_{2}}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}

around the configuration
\Gamma_{2}
. In fact, the top level surface certifying
this for the point
\gamma_{1}(\varepsilon)
is the bottom cylinder rescaled
by
1/(1-\varepsilon)
.

3.5.
Coordinates adapted to the principal boundary

For the remainder of this section, we make our standing hypothesis that
\mathcal{M}
has REL zero. We fix a principal graph
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{M}
. We also fix a suitable basis of relative homology that allows for volume comparisons at the boundary and near the boundary using the normalization in Section
3.2
. Let

\rho\coloneqq\operatorname{rank}{{\mathcal{M}}}\;=\;\frac{m+1}{2}

and let
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
be a component of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
with configuration
\Gamma
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For any flat surface
(X,\omega)
near a point in
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
, there exists a subset

\mathfrak{B}\,:=\,\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\rho}\}\,\subset\,H_{1}(X,Z(\omega),\mathbb{R})

such that the corresponding periods
a_{i}=\int_{\alpha_{i}}\omega
and

b_{i}=\int_{\beta_{i}}\omega
are local coordinates of
{\mathcal{M}}
with the
following properties:

i)

The periods
a_{1,j}
of the core curves
\alpha_{1,j}
of the

{\mathcal{M}}
-parallel cylinders that correspond to the horizontal edges on lower level (called the
horizontal core curves
) are multiples of the first basis
element
a_{1,j}=\mathfrak{a}_{j}a_{1}
for some constants
\mathfrak{a}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
and
all
j=1,\ldots,q
.

ii)

There are relative cycles
\beta_{1,j}
joining a singularity on
either side of each horizontal cylinder (the
horizontal crossing cycles
)
such that
b_{1,j}:=\int_{\beta_{1,j}}\omega=\mathfrak{b}_{j}b_{1}
for some
constants
\mathfrak{b}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
and
j=1,\ldots,q
.

iii)

The form
\vartheta
defined by (
11
)
and (
12
) is the imaginary part of the area form
h
.

iv)

The set
\mathfrak{B}
is symplectic, i.e.,
\langle\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
and
\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{\rho}\}
consists of absolute periods supported on the top level.

Proof.

The first set of equations expresses the
{\mathcal{M}}
-parallelity of
the cylinders and we can take
\alpha_{1}
to be an
\mathbb{R}
-multiple of one of
these core curves. The second set of equations is a restatement of
the additional condition for principal graphs in the case of REL zero. For the third statement,
we revisit the proof in
[
Ngu25
, Section 6.1]
of the existence of a basis

T_{(X,\omega)}{\mathcal{M}}

that satisfies iii). This merely requires that the extension of the symplectic
form to a hermitian form is diagonal of signature
(\rho,\rho)
in the
basis (
12
). We rescale the above
\alpha_{1}
and
\beta_{1}

so that the form is diagonal
(1,-1)
with respect to the corresponding
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
,
run the Gram–Schmidt process to find the remaining
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
, and
invert (
12
) to retrieve the desired
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
.
The last statement is a reformulation of the conclusion from
Lemma
3.2
.
∎

The following lemma explains why one can simply describe the projection to the boundary for
affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero, neither requiring the “slit construction” and “transporting
a hole” as in
[
EMZ03
]
nor requiring modification differentials as in
[
BCGGM19
]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose that the affine invariant submanifold
\mathcal{M}
has REL zero and
\Gamma
is
a principal graph. Then the residue of a multi-scale differential at a
vertical edge of any boundary point
p=(Y,{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
in
D_{\Gamma}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}}
is zero.

Proof.

To explain the basic idea, we fix a vertical edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
and suppose there exists a relative cycle
\gamma
on a flat surface
(X,\omega)
near the boundary point
p
such that
\gamma
crosses (the annulus corresponding to)
e
but no other degenerating annulus. By Lemma
3.4
, the period of
\gamma
can be expressed as a linear combination of the periods of
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\rho}
. By construction, these periods do not change along a loop around the boundary divisor corresponding to the vertical level passage. Since on the other hand the period of
\gamma
changes by
\ell/\kappa_{e}
times the residue along such a loop, the residue must be zero.

For the general case, consider any component
v\in V(\Gamma)
on the top level.
By the global residue condition, we have
\sum_{e\mapsto v}r_{e}=0
, where the sum is
over all edges adjacent to
v
, and where we take residues as integrals over
the core curves of the annuli connecting the top level to the bottom level oriented counterclockwise as boundary curves of the top level. Since any differential on any component of the bottom level has a zero, for any pair of edges
e_{i},e_{j}\mapsto v
, there is a relative period crossing
e_{i}
and
e_{j}
but no other
horizontal nor vertical edges. The preceding argument and the orientation
convention imply that
\ell(r_{e_{i}}/\kappa_{e_{i}}-r_{e_{j}}/\kappa_{e_{j}})=0
.
Since
\kappa_{e_{i}}>0
for all vertical edges, together with the global residue condition this implies that
r_{e_{i}}=0
for all
i
.
∎

3.6.
The parameter space for cylinders

We add more notations for the cylinders in a flat surface near the boundary of a principal
graph
\Gamma
that we fix throughout. For convenience we label the
cylinders by increasing order of periods of their waist
curves. Write
a_{1}=we^{i\theta}
and decompose
h=\operatorname{Im}(b_{1}e^{-i\theta})

and
t=\operatorname{Re}(b_{1}e^{-i\theta})
so that the cylinders have

\text{waist length}\,\,w_{j}\;=\;{\mathfrak{a}_{j}}w\,,\quad\text{height}\,\,h_{j}\;=\;{\mathfrak{b}_{j}}h\,,\quad\text{and twist}\,\,t_{j}\;=\;{\mathfrak{b}_{j}}t

respectively. The symplectic normalization of
\mathfrak{B}
implies that

A\,:=\,\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathfrak{a}_{j}\mathfrak{b}_{j}=1\,,

i.e., the total area covered by the cylinders is
A|a_{1}\wedge b_{1}|=|a_{1}\wedge b_{1}|=wh
.

The next result illustrates that surfaces near the principal boundary behave like Teichmüller curves.

Lemma 3.6
.

The ratios
\mathfrak{b}_{j}/\mathfrak{a}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,q
are commensurable.

Proof.

Consider the
j
-th shortest cylinders on two surfaces
in
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
with directions
\theta
resp.
\theta^{\prime}
, with
waist length
w_{j}
resp.
w_{j}^{\prime}
and crossing periods
\mathfrak{b}_{j}b_{1}

resp.
\mathfrak{b}_{j}b_{1}^{\prime}
. They are isomorphic as cylinders with the zeros on their boundary marked if and only if two conditions hold. First, if
w_{j}=w_{j}^{\prime}
, hence
a_{1}=a_{1}^{\prime}
. Second, if
\mathfrak{b}_{j}(b_{1}-b_{1}^{\prime})/\mathfrak{a}_{j}a_{1}
lies in a finite number of cosets of
\mathbb{Z}
, where the finite number stems from automorphisms permuting the zeros on their boundary. This means that the cylinders are isomorphic only if

\frac{b_{1}-b_{1}^{\prime}}{a_{1}}\in\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{j}}{R_{j}\mathfrak{b}_{j}}\cdot\mathbb{Z}\qquad\text{for some $R_{j}\in\mathbb{N}$}

that gives the order of cyclic permutations of the zeros at the boundaries.

Suppose the claim is wrong. Then for
a_{1}
in a small neighborhood of
an initial value
a_{1,0}
and all
b_{1}
such that
b_{1}/a_{1}
runs over
all
\mathbb{R}
-translates of a neigborhood of an initial value
b_{1,0}/a_{1,0}
,
it never happens that all the cylinders
\{C_{j}\}_{j=1}^{q}
are isomorphic.
This contradicts the finiteness of the volume of the affine invariant submanifold as
we can vary the parameters of an initial top level surface in a small open
neighborhood (thus preserving the conditions of belonging to the thick part)
and independently vary
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
as prescribed. If two surfaces in this
set were isomorphic, the cylinders
\{C_{j}\}_{j=1}^{q}
would have to be isomorphic,
as they are distinguished by the lengths of the core curves. Since this has been
ruled out, the
\mathbb{R}
-translates of
b_{1}/a_{1}
ensure that the volume is infinite.
∎

In the sequel, we fix the local “rotation number”
R_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
of the preceding
proof to be maximal such that a
(1/R_{j})
-th Dehn twist around its core curve
provides an automorphism of the
j
-th cylinder (with the zeros on its boundary
marked). Define

(22)

\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}\coloneqq\operatorname{lcm}\Bigl\{\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{j}}{R_{j}\mathfrak{b}_{j}},\,\,j=1,\ldots,q\Bigr\}\,.

We let
\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}
be the space of parameters of the
q
cylinders
that are
\mathcal{M}
-parallel, with relative sizes as above,
of total area one and such that at least one cylinder has a
waist curve of size
\leq\varepsilon
when rescaled by the total area. That is,

\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}\;=\;\big\{(a_{1},b_{1})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\,:\,|a_{1}\wedge b_{1}|=1,\,\,\operatorname{Re}\Bigl(\frac{b_{1}}{a_{1}}\Bigr)\in[0,\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}],\,\,\mathfrak{a}_{1}|a_{1}|\leq\varepsilon\big\}\,.

It will also be useful to consider

\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon,\leq 1}\;=\;\big\{(a_{1},b_{1})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\,:\,|a_{1}\wedge b_{1}|\leq 1,\,\,\operatorname{Re}\Bigl(\frac{b_{1}}{a_{1}}\Bigr)\in[0,\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}],\,\,\mathfrak{a}_{1}|a_{1}|\leq\varepsilon\big\}\,.

For a subset
U
in the hypersurface of flat surfaces of area one, we denote by
C_{s}(U)
the cone
under
U
of area bounded by
s
, i.e.,

(23)

C_{s}(U)\;=\;\{(X,\omega):\operatorname{vol}_{\omega}(X)\leq s,\,\,(X,\omega)/\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}_{\omega}(X)}\in U\}\,

and we omit the subscript for
s=1
.

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon,\leq 1}
is different from
C(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})
and that it contains in particular all
r\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}/r}
for
r\in(0,1]
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,\varepsilon]
. Finally we let

(24)

R\;=\;\{(r_{\top},r_{C})\in(0,1)^{2}\,:\,r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}<1\}

be the space parameterizing the relative sizes of the top level and
the cylinders.

3.7.
Components of the principal boundary

Here we want to decompose surfaces in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
further in their upper level and lower level in order to compute
c_{\star}({\mathcal{M}},\Gamma)
via the product decomposition. Let
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
be a connected component of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
with configuration
\Gamma
. Recall the maps
p_{\Gamma}
and
c_{\Gamma}
in Diagram (
4
).

Definition 3.7
.

Define
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\top}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\bot}
to be the linear submanifolds obtained by taking the Zariski closure of the top level projection and bottom level projection of
p_{\Gamma}(c_{\Gamma}^{-1}(\overline{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}}\cap D_{\Gamma}))
. Moreover, define
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}
to be the intersection of
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\top}
with the (real) hypersurface of flat surfaces of area one (with our standing volume normalization thanks to Lemma
3.2
).

By definition of principal graphs, the projectivized stratum
{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\bot}
is a finite set. Also by the assumption that
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
is connected, indeed
{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\bot}
consists of a single point which parameterizes a unique bottom differential up to scaling.

Proposition 3.8
.

There exists a connected subset
\mathcal{F}^{\top}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}
and a connected subset
\overline{\mathcal{F}}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
with a finite covering
\mathcal{F}\to\overline{\mathcal{F}}
of degree
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}(\Gamma)
such that:

(i)

The top level subset has almost full volume
\nu_{\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\top}}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{\top})=o(\varepsilon^{2})
.

(ii)

The subset in the stratum has almost full volume

\nu_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{F}})=o(\varepsilon^{2})
.

(iii)

There is a map
\sigma\colon C(\mathcal{F})\to C(\mathcal{F}^{\top})\times\mathcal{P}_{{\varepsilon},\leq 1}
that preserves the periods
a_{1},\ldots,b_{\rho}
. The image of
\sigma
fibers over
R
under the map taking square root of the areas of both factors, and the
fiber over
(r_{\top},r_{C})
is
{r_{\top}}\mathcal{F}^{\top}\times r_{C}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C},}
. Moreover, the map
\sigma
is a covering over its image.

We need to record the degree of these maps for later use:

Definition 3.9
.

Given a connected principal boundary component
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
, define the number of
reachable decorations
for
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
to be

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}=\deg(\sigma)/|\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}(\Gamma)|
.

Note that
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}
is well-defined, as the degree of
\sigma
is constant on
\mathcal{F}
since we have assumed that these full measure sets are connected.
It comprises choices of reachable prong-matching equivalence classes (in the sense of
[
CMS23a
]
), rescaled by the size of the graph automorphism subgroup preserving the linear submanifold, in fact

(25)

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}=\frac{K^{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}{|\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}(\Gamma)|\,\ell_{\Gamma}}

by (
8
).

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

As a first approximation for the set
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
, we take
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
, which we recall to be defined as a component of the thick part in the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood given by the bullet points in the proof of Proposition
3.3
.

To define the second factor of
\sigma
, the map to
\mathcal{P}_{{\varepsilon},\leq 1}
, choose
a basis of homology as in Lemma
3.4
. Note that
\alpha_{1}
is
globally well-defined if we scale it so that
\mathfrak{a}_{1}=1
(and scale
\beta_{1}

by the inverse to preserve condition iv)). Moreover,
\beta_{1}
is well-defined among
all choices satisfying condition ii) up to adding
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}\alpha_{1}
by the
argument leading to the definition (
22
). Thus
\beta_{1}

is unique with the requirement that
\operatorname{Re}(b_{1}/a_{1})\in[0,\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma})
. We take
(a_{1},b_{1})
with this choice of basis. To see that this defines indeed a map to
\mathcal{P}_{{\varepsilon},\leq 1}
, observer that
|a_{1}\wedge b_{1}|
is now the total area of the space covered by cylinders, which is indeed less than one. The last condition in
\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon,\leq 1}
reflects the length of the shortest cylinder waist curve.

To define the first factor of
\sigma
, we replace
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
by a connected and simply connected set of full measure and pass to a covering
\mathcal{F}\to\overline{\mathcal{F}}
where all the edges of
\Gamma
are marked. This can be achieved by a map of degree
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\Gamma)
. We now define as approximation for
\sigma
a map to
C(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top})
. Informally, the boundary components of the
tubular neighborhoods
T
from the proof of Proposition
3.3
can be
embedded into small discs (since the residues are zero by Lemma
3.5
), so we fill in those discs. Formally, in order to also determine where to place the zero in the disc, we proceed similarly as in the proof of that lemma.

Recall from
[
Ben23
]
how the boundary of a linear submanifold is cut out locally, given the equations of the ambient linear submanifold in period coordinates. We only care about the top level and use the absence of horizontal nodes on top level to simplify the procedure. The recipe is to put equations in reduced row echelon form, suppress any appearance of lower level variables, and replace each cycle
\gamma
by its top level part
\gamma^{\top}
cutting it off at the nodes to lower level. By Lemma
3.4
, we can take the
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
there for
i\geq 2
as pivot variables for the echelon form. This means that we can solve the equations uniquely for the missing absolute and relative periods in
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}
. The last
condition in the definition of the thick part ensures that we can find a surface with the required periods by shrinking the short saddle connections. This procedure indeed does not depend on the chosen basis, since
\alpha_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
are suppressed in the equations and since a symplectic base change among the other variables can be accounted for by the corresponding base change on the top level surface.

The claim that the image of
\sigma
fibers over
R
follows since the first factor of
\sigma
did not change the volume of the top level surface, because the volume is computed in terms of absolute periods. The claim that it fibers over
{r_{\top}}\mathcal{F}^{\top}\times r_{C}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C}}
is now obvious, since the shortest period of a core curve in a surface parameterized by
C(\mathcal{F})
with image
(r_{\top},r_{C})\in R
is bounded by
\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}
.

We define
\mathcal{F}^{\top}
to be the thick part of
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}
where the length
of the shortest saddle connection is at least
4W\varepsilon
minus the boundary
\operatorname{Re}(b_{1}/a_{i})=0
. This set certainly satisfies (ii). We claim that the
\sigma
-image contains
{r_{\top}}\mathcal{F}^{\top}\times r_{C}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C},}
for any
(r_{\top},r_{C})\in R
and
\varepsilon
small enough.
In fact, fix the reachable decorations (prong-matching and lower level surface)
that occur in the closure of
\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_{1,\Gamma}
and invert the procedure in
the definition of
\sigma
: Replace discs around the zeros of the top level surface
by the lower level surface, with the infinite strips corresponding to horizontal edges replaced by cylinders according to the parameter in
\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C},}
.
Formally, again, we use the equations of the linear submanifold (and REL zero)
to revert the above procedure. This procedure changes periods by at most
W\varepsilon

so that the surface we construct lies in
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}
. We let
\mathcal{F}
be the
\sigma
-preimage of
\mathcal{F}^{\top}
. It contains the thick part where the shortest saddle connection not at the boundary of any
C_{j}
has length at least

5W\varepsilon
and thus satisfies (i). The same argument as in the construction of preimages shows that
\sigma
is a covering over its image.
∎

4.
Disintegration of the volume forms

We continue to assume that
{\mathcal{M}}
has REL zero in this section. The goal of this section is to convert the “almost-period preserving bijection” from Proposition
3.8
into an “almost measure equality” that we give in its first form in Corollary
4.2
. As a second step, we perform integration with Siegel–Veech weights and obtain a formula for the Siegel–Veech constants in terms of boundary contributions.

Consider the Lebesque measure and its disintegration. We define

(26)

\nu_{P}\;=\;\left(\frac{\bf i}{2}\right)^{2}da_{1}\,d\overline{a}_{1}\,db_{1}\,d\overline{b}_{1}\;=\;r^{3}\,dr\,d\nu^{P}_{\rm dis}

on the cylinder parameter space
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
. Obviously, the volume
\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}):=\nu^{P}_{\rm dis}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})
is finite. In fact, we have:

Lemma 4.1
.

The volume of the parameter space
\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}
is given by

(27)

\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})\;=\;\dim_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})\int_{C(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})}\nu_{P}\;=\;\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}}\cdot 2\pi\varepsilon^{2}\,.

Proof.

The first equality is another instance of the comparison in (
18
).

Recall that the Lebesgue measure decomposes into
\nu_{P}=d\theta\,w\,dw\,dh\,dt
where
a_{1}=we^{i\theta},h=\operatorname{Im}(b_{1}e^{-i\theta})
, and
t=\operatorname{Re}(b_{1}e^{-i\theta})
. Hence,
|a_{1}\wedge b_{1}|=wh
,
\operatorname{Re}\left(\frac{b_{1}}{a_{1}}\right)=\frac{t}{w}
, and
C(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})
is then parameterized by

C(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})=\left\{(\theta,w,h,t)\in[0,2\pi)\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}:\,wh\leq 1,\frac{t}{w}\in[0,\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}],\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{1}w}{\sqrt{wh}}<\varepsilon\right\}\,.

In this coordinate system, we compute the volume of the parameter space to be

\displaystyle\phantom{\;=\;}\int_{C(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})}\nu_{P}\;=\;\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}wh\leq 1\\
\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}w\leq\varepsilon^{2}h\end{subarray}}\left(\int_{0}^{\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}w}dt\right)w\,dw\,dh\;=\;2\pi\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}wh\leq 1\\
\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}w\leq\varepsilon^{2}h\end{subarray}}w^{2}\,dw\,dh

\displaystyle\;=\;2\pi\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}\left(\int_{0}^{\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{w\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}h}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}}}w^{2}dw\right)dh+\int_{\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}{\varepsilon}}^{\infty}\left(\int_{w\leq\frac{1}{h}}w^{2}dw\right)dh\right)\;=\;\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\,.

∎

Recall that
\rho=(m+1)/2
is the rank of
{\mathcal{M}}
. Then the parametrization of the components of
{\mathcal{M}}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{thick}}
implies:

Corollary 4.2
.

The measure of any component of the
\varepsilon
-thick part with
configuration
\Gamma
can be estimated as

(28)

\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_{1,\Gamma})\;=\;\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}16}(m-1)(m+1)}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top})\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})+o(\varepsilon^{2})

as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

Let
\vartheta
be the
2
-form in (
11
)
obtained from the coordinates in Lemma
3.4
. We define

(29)

\vartheta^{\top}\coloneqq\frac{{{\bf i}}}{2}\Big(\sum_{i=2}^{\rho}d{A_{i}}\wedge d\bar{A_{i}}-d{B_{i}}\wedge d\bar{B_{i}}\Big)\,

as the corresponding
2
-form omitting the first pair of variables that corresponds to the bottom level. Then a routine calculation shows that

(30)

\displaystyle\nu_{\rm int}=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\bf i}\vartheta)}^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}\;=\;\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}^{3m+3}}}{2^{2m+2}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{\rho}da_{i}\wedge d\overline{a}_{i}\wedge db_{i}\wedge d\overline{b}_{i}\,,

\displaystyle\nu^{\top}_{\rm int}=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\bf i}\vartheta\top)}^{m-1}}{(m-1)!}\;=\;\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}^{3m-3}}}{2^{2m-2}}\right)\prod_{i=2}^{\rho}da_{i}\wedge d\overline{a}_{i}\wedge db_{i}\wedge d\overline{b}_{i}\,.

Recall from Lemma
3.4
iii) and a similar volume normalization on the top level. We have

(31)

\nu_{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}}(B)\;=\;\int_{C(B)}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\bf i}\vartheta)}^{m+1}}{(m+1)!}\,,\qquad\nu_{{\mathcal{M}}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}}(B^{\prime})\;=\;\int_{C(B^{\prime})}{\frac{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}}\vartheta^{\top})^{m-1}}{(m-1)!}\,}

for measurable sets
B\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
B^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}

contained in any chart, where the expressions for the differential forms are
defined and where
C(\cdot)
denotes the cones under the hypersurface of area-one flat surfaces
in both cases. We apply this to
\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_{1,\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top}

(covering the whole space by appropriate charts and adding contributions)
and the map of cones
\sigma
from Proposition
3.8
on the almost full measure sets defined there. We then obtain that

(32)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_{1,\Gamma})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:defmeasure},\eqref{eq:volrelation},\eqref{eq:wedgeproduct}}}{{{\;=\;}}}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})\int_{C(\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_{1,\Gamma})}\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}^{3m+3}}}{2^{2m+2}}\right)da_{1}\cdots\,d\overline{b}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\;\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})\int_{\operatorname{Im}(\sigma)}\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}^{3m+3}}}{2^{2m+2}}\right)da_{1}\cdots\,d\overline{b}_{\rho}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:wedgeproduct}}}{{\;=\;}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})\frac{{\bf i}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}6-2}}}{2^{2}}\int_{\operatorname{Im}(\sigma)}d\nu_{P}d\nu_{\rm int}^{\top}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:defnudis},\eqref{eq:Pdis}}}{{{\;=\;}}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{2^{2}}}\int_{R}\Bigl(r_{\top}^{2m-3}\int_{\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{1,\Gamma}}d\nu_{\rm dis}\,\cdot

\displaystyle r_{C}^{3}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C}}}\!d\nu^{P}_{\rm dis}\Bigr)\,\,\,dr_{\top}d{r_{C}}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:volrelation},\eqref{eq:volPdef}}}{{{\;=\;}}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{2^{2}}}\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{1,\Gamma})\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})\cdot

\displaystyle\int_{R}\left(\frac{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}{r_{C}^{2}}\right)r_{\top}^{2m-3}r_{C}^{3}\,dr_{\top}d{r_{C}}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\;\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}16}(m-1)(m+1)}\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{1,\Gamma})\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})+o(\varepsilon^{2})\,,

where we used the equality

(33)

\int_{R}r_{\top}^{2a-1}r_{C}^{2b-1}dr_{\top}dr_{C}\;=\;\frac{1}{4}\frac{(a-1)!(b-1)!}{(a+b)!}\,

for the last line and to derive the fourth line we used that
\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{P}_{c\cdot\varepsilon})=c^{2}\cdot\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})
by Lemma
4.1
, with specifically
c=\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C}
.
∎

To compute area or cylinder Siegel–Veech constants, note that they only depend
on short closed geodesics in cylinders and are thus controlled by the point
in the parameter space
\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}
. Therefore, we introduce the following weight
functions. Recall that
a_{1}=we^{i\theta}
, let
h_{i}=\operatorname{Im}(b_{i}e^{-i\theta})
,
and note that
\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathfrak{a}_{j}h_{i}=h
. The volume of the cylinder
space can be written equivalently as
|a_{1}\wedge b_{1}|=wh
. Letting
\mathfrak{a}_{q+1}:=\infty
, we define

(34)

\displaystyle W^{\varepsilon}_{\star}(w,h)

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{i=1}^{q}\chi(w\mathfrak{a}_{i}\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{wh}\leq w\mathfrak{a}_{i+1})f_{\star}(i)

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{i=1}^{q}\chi\left(\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq h\leq\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)f_{\star}(i)\,

for
\star\in\{\operatorname{area},\operatorname{cyl}\}
, where

f_{\operatorname{area}}(i)\;=\;\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathfrak{a}_{j}\mathfrak{b}_{j}\,,\quad f_{\operatorname{cyl}}(i)\;=\;i\,.

Note that
f_{\operatorname{area}}(q)=1
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The Siegel–Veech weighted volume of the parameter space
associated with
\Gamma
for
\star\in\{\operatorname{area},\operatorname{cyl}\}
is

(35)

\operatorname{Vol}_{\star}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})\coloneqq\dim_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})\int_{C(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})}W^{\varepsilon}_{\star}(w,h)d\nu_{P}\;=\;2\pi\varepsilon^{2}c_{\Gamma}^{\star}

with

c_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{area}}\;=\;{\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\frac{\mathfrak{b}_{j}}{\mathfrak{a}_{j}}\,,\qquad c_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{cyl}}\;=\;\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{2}}\,.

Proof.

Since the weights neither depend on the twist parameters
t_{i}
nor on the
argument of
a_{i}
, and since all functions involved are positive, we can integrate as in Lemma
4.1
:

\displaystyle\int_{C(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})}W^{\varepsilon}_{\star}(w,h)d\nu_{P}

\displaystyle=\;\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}wh\leq 1\\
\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}w\leq\varepsilon^{2}h\end{subarray}}\left(\int_{0}^{\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}w}dt\right)W^{\varepsilon}_{\star}(w,h)w\,dw\,dh

\displaystyle=\;2\pi\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}wh\leq 1\\
\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}w\leq\varepsilon^{2}h\end{subarray}}W^{\varepsilon}_{\star}(w,h)w^{2}\,dw\,dh\,.

This last integral is the sum over
i\in\{1,\dots,q\}
of the following
integrals

\displaystyle I^{\star}_{i}

\displaystyle\;=\;f_{\star}(i)\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}wh\leq 1\\
\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{2}w\leq\varepsilon^{2}h\end{subarray}}w^{2}\chi\left(\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq h\leq\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\,dw\,dh

\displaystyle\;=\;f_{\star}(i)\int_{w=0}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}}}w^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq h\leq\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\,dh\right)\,dw

\displaystyle\quad+f_{\star}(i)\int_{w=\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}}}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}w^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq h\leq\frac{1}{w}\right)\,dh\right)\,dw

\displaystyle\;=\;f_{\star}(i)\int_{0}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}}}w^{2}\left(\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}-\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\,dw+f_{\star}(i)\int_{\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}}}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}w^{2}\left(\frac{1}{w}-\frac{w\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\,dw

\displaystyle\;=\;f_{\star}(i)\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\left(\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}^{2}}\right)\,,

where the first decomposition comes from the domain integration illustrated in Figure
2
. Combining the two computations gives the claim.
∎

H
w
\cfrac{\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}^{2}w}{\varepsilon^{2}}
\cfrac{\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}w}{\varepsilon^{2}}
\cfrac{\mathfrak{a}_{1}w}{\varepsilon^{2}}
\cfrac{1}{w}
\cfrac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}}
\cfrac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}

Figure 2.
Integration domain in the variables
w
and
H

Now we can explicitly compute the local Siegel–Veech constants
c_{\star}(\mathcal{M},\Gamma)
in Proposition
3.3
by adapting the computations of Corollary
4.2
with the Siegel–Veech weight.

Proposition 4.4
.

The local Siegel–Veech constants are given by

c_{\star}(\mathcal{M},\Gamma)\;=\;\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}}c_{\star}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})

with

c_{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})\;=\;\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}8}(m-1)(m+1)}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\cdot c_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{cyl}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top})}{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})}

and

c_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})=\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}8}(m-1)m(m+1)}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\cdot c_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{area}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top})}{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})}

where
c_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{area}}
and
c_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{cyl}}
are defined in Lemma
4.3
.

We remark that in the case of
\mathcal{M}
being the minimal strata of Abelian differentials, there is only one cylinder pinched in the principal boundary, i.e.,
q=1
. In this case, due to the normalization choice of
\mathfrak{a}_{1}=\mathfrak{b}_{1}=1
, the above formulas imply immediately
c_{\Gamma}^{\rm area}=c_{\Gamma}^{\rm cyl}
for all
\Gamma
, and consequently
c_{\rm area}(\mathcal{M})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{M})\cdot c_{\rm cyl}(\mathcal{M})
for the minimal strata. Indeed, this relation holds for all strata of Abelian differentials, originally due to Vorobets
[
Vor97
, Theorem 1.6]
and further explained to us by Zorich. The relation also holds for strata of quadratic differentials due to
[
Gou15
]
. However, this relation does not hold for a number of special affine invariant submanifolds, e.g., for many Teichmüller curves, starting already with the
L
-shaped surface consisting of three squares. It would be interesting to characterize affine invariant submanifolds
\mathcal{M}
for which
c_{\rm area}(\mathcal{M})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{M})\cdot c_{\rm cyl}(\mathcal{M})
holds.

Proof.

We use the definition of
c_{\star}({\mathcal{M}},\Gamma)
given in (
21
) and decompose
\operatorname{Vol}_{\star}(\mathcal{M}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})
into the sum of the volumes of the connected components
\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
, which gives the first equation. We then compute the weighted volumes

\operatorname{Vol}_{\star}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})\;=\;\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})\cdot\int_{\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}}W_{\star}(S)\,d\nu_{\mathcal{M}}(S)

using Proposition
3.8
as in Corollary
4.2
. The weights
W_{\star}(S)
on the fiber
{r_{\top}}\mathcal{F}^{\top}\times r_{C}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C}
are given by

W_{\operatorname{area}}(S)\;=\;W_{\operatorname{area}}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(w,h)\cdot\frac{r_{\top}^{2}}{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad W_{\operatorname{cyl}}(S)\;=\;W_{\operatorname{cyl}}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(w,h)\,,

with
W_{\star}^{\varepsilon}(w,h)
given by (
34
). For the area weight, we
then obtain as in (
32
) that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=\;}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})\int_{C(\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_{1,\Gamma})}W_{\operatorname{area}}(S)\,\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}^{3m+3}}}{2^{2m+2}}\right)da_{1}\cdots\,d\overline{b}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\;\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}{2}\right)^{2}\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{1,\Gamma})\,\int_{R}\Big(\operatorname{Vol}_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}/r_{C}})\,\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{r_{\top}^{2}}{r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2}}r_{\top}^{2m-1}r_{C}^{3}\Big)\,dr_{\top}\,d{r_{C}}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\;\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}{2}\right)^{2}\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{1,\Gamma})\operatorname{Vol}_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})\int_{R}r_{\top}^{2m-1}r_{C}^{3}\,dr_{\top}\,d{r_{C}}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\;\frac{\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}16}(m-1)m(m+1)}\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{1,\Gamma})\operatorname{Vol}_{\operatorname{area}}(\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})+o(\varepsilon^{2})\,

where the last equality follows from (
33
).
A similar computation shows that the integral with
W_{\operatorname{cyl}}(S)
produces the
same expression without
{r_{\top}^{2}}/(r_{\top}^{2}+r_{C}^{2})
. As in (
32
)
the
R
-integral gives
1/4(m-1)(m+1)
instead of
1/{4(m-1)m(m+1)}
.
The claim now follows by inserting (
35
) and
the fundamental limit formula (
21
).
∎

5.
Siegel–Veech constants as intersection numbers

In this section, we will show that the right-hand side of Formula (
2
) matches the flat geometric contribution to
c_{\operatorname{area}}({\mathcal{M}})
from the principal boundary strata of
{\mathcal{M}}
as described in Propositions
3.3
and
4.4
. Recall that
m=\dim\mathbb{P}\mathcal{M}
.
The case of
m=1
corresponds to Teichmüller curves for which the conjectural formula has been known to hold. From now on we assume that
m\geq 2
and we still assume that
{\mathcal{M}}
has zero REL zero.

We first reduce the intersection calculation in the numerator of the right-hand side of Formula (
2
) to the boundary divisor with horizontal nodes. Let
\delta
be the total boundary divisor of
{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}
and
\delta_{H}
the boundary divisor whose elements have horizontal nodes.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an affine invariant submanifold of REL zero. Denote by
\delta_{\Lambda}
a boundary divisor of
{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}
with a two-level
graph
\Lambda
whose generic elements have no horizontal nodes. Then

\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\delta_{\Lambda}\xi^{m-1}=0\quad{\rm and}\quad\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m-1}\delta=\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m-1}\delta_{H}\,.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\Lambda}^{\top}
be the unprojectivized top level stratum of
\delta_{\Lambda}
. We know that

2\penalty\ {\rm rank}\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{\Lambda}^{\top}\leq\dim\mathcal{M}_{\Lambda}^{\top}<\dim\mathcal{M}=m+1=2\rho

where we recall that
\rho
denotes the rank of
\mathcal{M}
. It follows that
