{\rm rank}\penalty\ \mathcal{M}_{\Lambda}^{\top}\leq\rho-1
. Therefore, the subspace spanned by the absolute periods of
\mathcal{M}_{\Lambda}^{\top}
has dimension at most
m-1
, and hence after projectivization,
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\delta_{\Lambda}\xi^{m-1}=0
. The other identity then follows right away.
∎

Next, we want to apply the divisor class relation
[
CMS23a
, Proposition 4.1]
by taking one of the simple polar edges in each component of
\delta_{H}
to go down to the lower level of some resulting two-level graphs
\Gamma
. Note that this forces all horizontal edges that are
\mathcal{M}
-parallel to each other to go down simultaneously to the lower level of
\Gamma
. We claim that

(36)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m-1}\delta_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\cdot c^{\rm area}_{\Gamma}\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\top}}\xi^{m-2}

where the summation runs over all irreducible components
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
of the codimension-two boundary strata associated to the graphs
\Gamma
, the factor
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}
is given in Definition
3.9
, and the factor
c_{\Gamma}^{\rm area}
is defined in Lemma
4.3
.

The presence of the factor
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}
follows from the same reason as in Corollary
4.2
. To justify the presence of the factor
c_{\Gamma}^{\rm area}
, consider a loop in
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
once around the intersection with the horizontal boundary divisor. Computing the number of times this twists around each branch of
\delta_{H}
that passes through such a boundary point and adding up this contribution computes the intersection multiplicity. Recall the coordinate system near the boundary from Section
3.5
and in particular the coordinates for the cylinders from Section
3.6
. Fixing
a_{i}
and
\operatorname{Im}(b_{1}/a_{1})
while letting
\operatorname{Re}(b_{1}/a_{1})
run along
[0,\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}]
is actually a closed loop and once around the boundary by the choice of
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}
as
\operatorname{lcm}
in (
22
). This loop induces
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}/(\mathfrak{a}_{j}/\mathfrak{b}_{j})
times a
(1/R_{j})
-fractional Dehn twist around the
j
-th cylinder. Since each such fractional Dehn twist contributes one to the intersection number, the claim follows.

Note that
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma}}\xi^{m-2}\neq 0
only if the projectivized dimension of the bottom stratum
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
is zero. In this case,
\Gamma
is precisely a principal graph as defined in Section
3.3
where the bottom stratum
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Y}^{\bot}_{\Gamma}
consists of a single point due to the irreducibility assumption of
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
. This explains the notation
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
which corresponds to
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
defined in Definition
3.7
as
\varepsilon
tends to zero.

Combining the above discussions, we conclude that the intersection contribution of each component
\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}
of the principal boundary to
c_{\rm area}(\mathcal{M})
is given by

(37)

\displaystyle c_{\rm area}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathcal{M},\Gamma)\,:=\,-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot c_{\Gamma}^{\rm area}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}}\cdot\frac{\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\top}}\xi^{m-2}}{\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m}}\,.

Now we are ready to prove the main result.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Comparing Proposition
4.4
to (
37
), it suffices to check that

(38)

\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M})}{2(m-1)m(m+1)}\cdot\left(\frac{\bf i}{2}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top})}{\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})}=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma}^{\top}}\xi^{m-2}}{\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m}}\,.

Recall from (
16
) that

\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{M})=\frac{2}{m!}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}}\pi)^{m+1}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{P}\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\xi^{m}\,

and similarly

\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Y}_{1,\Gamma}^{\top})=\frac{2}{(m-2)!}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bf i}}\pi)^{m-1}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma}}\xi^{m-2}\,.

Then (
38
) obviously holds.
∎

6.
Examples of principal graphs

In this section, we describe the principal boundary for several examples of affine invariant submanifolds of REL zero.

6.1.
The minimal strata of Abelian differentials

The principal boundary for strata of Abelian differentials was already known in
[
EMZ03
]
(see also
[
CC19
]
for the description in terms of twisted differentials). In the case of the minimal stratum in genus
g
with a unique zero, since every bottom vertex must contain at least one zero, the bottom of a principal graph
\Gamma
consists of a unique vertex
(X_{0},\omega_{0})
which admits a horizontal loop and connects to the top vertices
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
via
k
separating edges. In particular,
(X_{0},\omega_{0})
belongs to the generalized stratum in genus zero with signature
(2g-2,-1,-1,-2g_{1},\ldots,-2g_{k})^{\mathfrak{R}}
, where the residue condition
\mathfrak{R}
requires the residue of
\omega_{0}
to be zero at each vertical polar edge. Moreover, each top vertex
(X_{i},\omega_{i})
belongs to a minimal stratum with signature
(2g_{i}-2)
, where
g_{1}+\cdots+g_{k}=g-1
. See Figure
3
for an illustration of such a principal graph.

g_{1}
g_{2}
g_{k}
\shortminus 2g_{1}

2g_{1}-2

\shortminus 2g_{2}

2g_{2}-2

\,\,\,\shortminus 2g_{k}

\,\,\,2g_{k}-2

2g-2

Figure 3.
The principal graph of the minimal stratum
\mathcal{H}(2g-2)

6.2.
Strata of quadratic differentials with odd orders of zeros only

The principal boundary for strata of quadratic differentials was studied in
[
MZ08
]
and
[
Gou15
]
. Note that a stratum of quadratic differentials has REL zero if and only if the order of every zero is odd. For instance, the principal boundary configurations for the strata of quadratic differentials with simple zeros and poles were completely described in
[
Gou15
, Section 4]
. Since this paper uses different drawing conventions, we draw the principal graphs in terms of level graphs of multi-scale
quadratic
differentials
in the case when the number of singularities is large enough. (If the number of singularities is small, there exist exceptional configurations which are not included below.)

In these schematic pictures, a vertex with a plus-sign indicates that the corresponding quadratic differential is a square of an Abelian differential, while a minus-sign indicates that the differential is strictly quadratic, i.e., not a global square of an Abelian differential. We do not indicate the zeros nor poles adjacent to the vertices, nor their genera of any of the vertices on the top level. The number of Abelian vertices can be arbitrary, zeros permitted, but the number of strictly quadratic vertices is either one or two, as indicated. Finally, using the (admissible) canonical double cover of these quadratic differentials, one can also draw the principal graphs of the corresponding multi-scale
Abelian
differentials when lifting the strata of quadratic differentials as affine invariant submanifolds into the respective strata of Abelian differentials.

6.3.
The flex and gothic loci

We start with a brief recap on the gothic locus
\Omega\mathcal{G}
, an exceptional affine invariant submanifold found in
[
MMW17
]
, together with its companions, the flex locus
\mathcal{F}
and the corresponding locus of quadratic differentials
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F}
over
\mathcal{F}
, where
\Omega\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F}
can be identified through the canonical double covering construction (up to labeling the zeros and poles).

An example of a flat surface parameterized in the gothic locus is given in Figure
4
.

v_{4}

v_{3}

v_{2}

v_{1}

v_{6}

v_{5}

w_{1}

v_{1}

w_{6}

v_{6}

w_{5}

v_{5}

w_{4}

v_{4}

w_{3}

v_{3}

w_{2}

v_{2}

w_{4}

w_{3}

w_{2}

w_{1}

w_{6}

w_{5}

Figure 4.
A flat surface parameterized in the locus of cyclic forms inside the gothic locus (from
[
MMW17
, Figure 4]
)

For the intersection calculations on the flex locus, we will use the notation and results in
[
Che22
]
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{F}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,3}
be the closure of the flex locus parameterizing
(E,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
, where

E
admits a plane cubic model with a projection
\pi
from a point
s\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
, and
p_{3}
are three collinear cocritical points of
\pi
. Let
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1,3}(-1^{3},1^{3})
be the locus of quadratic differentials
(E,q)
over
\mathcal{F}
such that
\operatorname{div}(q)=z_{1}+z_{2}+z_{3}-p_{1}-p_{2}-p_{3}
, where
z_{1}+z_{2}+z_{3}
is a fiber of
\pi
. We have
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F}=4
. Note that we follow the setting of
[
Che22
]
for
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F}
, where
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
, and
p_{3}
are ordered but
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
, and
z_{3}
are unordered. Lifting via the canonical double cover identifies
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F}
with the gothic locus
\Omega\mathcal{G}\subset\Omega\mathcal{M}_{4,6}(2^{3},0^{3})
.
In order to keep it consistent with the setting in this paper, we label all the preimages of
p_{i}
and
z_{j}
in
\Omega\mathcal{G}
. Therefore,
{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\mathcal{G}\to{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F}
is a finite map of degree six, where each fiber consists of the choices of labeling the preimages of
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
, and
z_{3}
.
We also denote by
{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Q}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
the corresponding closures in the multi-scale compactification.

Combining the intersection numbers obtained in
[
Che22
]
with our main Theorem
1.1
, we restate and prove the part of Corollary
1.3
regarding the value of
c_{\rm area}(\Omega\mathcal{G})
as follows.

Corollary 6.1
.

The area Siegel–Veech constant of the gothic locus
\Omega\mathcal{G}
is

c_{\rm area}(\Omega\mathcal{G})\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{49}{13}\,.

Proof.

We can verify the claim in two ways. First, combining
[
Che22
, Theorem 1.2]
, the quadratic version of Theorem
1.1
as explained in
[
CMS23
]
, and
[
EKZ14
, Equation (2.4)]
, we find

L^{+}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F})\;=\;\frac{6}{13}\,,\quad L^{-}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F})\;=\;\frac{19}{13}\,,

where
L^{\pm}
stands for the respective sum of Lyapunov exponents. It follows that

L(\Omega\mathcal{G})\;=\;L^{+}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F})+L^{-}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{F})\;=\;\frac{25}{13}\,.

Now, using
[
EKZ14
, Equation (2.1)]
, we thus obtain

c_{\operatorname{area}}(\Omega\mathcal{G})\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{49}{13}\,.

Alternatively, we can use the intersection formula in Theorem
1.1
directly. Let
\eta
be the divisor class of the
\mathcal{O}(-1)
bundle on
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Q}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
. Then
[
Che22
, Theorem 1.2]
implies that

(40)

\int_{\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Q}\overline{\mathcal{F}}}\eta^{3}\;=\;-\frac{13}{6}\,,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Q}\overline{\mathcal{F}}}\eta^{2}\lambda_{1}\;=\;-\frac{1}{2}\,.

By the well-known relation of divisor classes
12\lambda=\kappa+\delta
on the moduli space of curves
and

\kappa=-\eta
(modulo non-horizontal boundary divisor classes) on
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Q}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
(see
[
Che19
, Proposition 2.1]
), we obtain

(41)

\int_{\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Q}\overline{\mathcal{F}}}\eta^{2}\delta\;=\;-\frac{49}{6}\,.

Next, we lift the above intersection calculations to the gothic locus
{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}
through the canonical double cover. In this process, note that
\eta
pulls back
to
-2\xi
, we label (the preimages of)
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
, and
z_{3}
in
{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}
but not in
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Q}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
, differentials in
{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}
has an involution automorphism of order two due to the double covering construction, and every horizontal node in the target of
an admissible double cover has two preimage nodes in the domain. Taking all of these into account, we thus obtain

(42)

\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}}\xi^{3}\;=\;\frac{13}{16}\,,\qquad\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}}\xi^{2}\delta\;=\;-\frac{49}{4}\,.

Finally, we conclude that

c_{\rm area}(\Omega\mathcal{G})\;=\;-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}}\xi^{2}\delta}{\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{G}}}\xi^{3}}\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{49}{13}\,

by using Theorem
1.1
.
∎

In the remaining part of this section, we will describe all the principal graphs for the gothic locus, which consist of four types and are labeled by I, II, III, and IV below.

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rm I}

This provides an alternative way to evaluate the area Siegel–Veech constant of
\Omega\mathcal{G}
by decomposing it into the respective contribution of each principal boundary component as in (
37
), thus proving the remaining part of Corollary
1.3
.

Proposition 6.2
.

For each type of the principal boundary components of
\Omega\mathcal{G}
, its contribution to
c_{\operatorname{area}}(\Omega\mathcal{G})
defined in (
37
) is:

(44)

\displaystyle c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm I})=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{15}{13}\,,\quad c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm II})=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{13}\,,

\displaystyle c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm III})=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{27}{13}\,,\quad c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm IV})=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{6}{13}\,.

In particular, they sum up to the value of
c_{\operatorname{area}}(\Omega\mathcal{G})
as in Corollary
6.1
.

Let
{\rm X}
denote one of the above four types. The strategy of evaluating
c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm X})
is the following. First, (
37
) reduces to

(45)

c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm X})=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot c_{\Gamma_{\rm X}}^{\operatorname{area}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm X}}}\cdot\frac{\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma_{\rm X}}}\xi}{\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\mathcal{\overline{G}}}\xi^{3}}\,.

Additionally, we know
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\mathcal{\overline{G}}}\xi^{3}=13/16
by (
42
). Moreover, for all the principal graphs of
\Omega\mathcal{G}
,
the number of reachable prong-matching equivalence classes (as part of the data to determine
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm X}}}
) is always
1
. Therefore, it remains to determine the factor
c_{\Gamma_{\rm X}}^{\operatorname{area}}
, the top-level degree
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma_{\rm X}}}\xi
, and the graph automorphism subgroup, all of which can be solved by using the explicit geometry of each principal graph.

We now discuss the cases of possible principal graphs and nickname them according to the shapes of the underlying two-level graphs without horizontal degeneration. There are two ways to prove that the list in (
43
) is complete. First, V. Delecroix has provided us with a list of boundary intersections obtained by searching the full boundary of the gothic locus using the sage-program
flatsurf
, which consists of those types we have found using admissible covers and degenerations studied by J. Schwab in
[
Sch24
]
. Alternatively, the sum of the area Siegel–Veech contributions in (
44
) is already equal to the value of
c_{\rm area}(\Omega\mathcal{G})
in Corollary
6.1
, which implies that there cannot be any other principal boundary contributions.

6.3.1.
Type I: The banana graph

In order to understand the top level, we use a flat geometric degeneration. We start from Figure
4
and recall from
[
MMW17
]
that the gothic locus is locally cut out by the equations

(46)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle v_{i}=-v_{i+3}\,,\quad w_{i}=-w_{i+3}\,,\qquad i=1,2,3

\displaystyle v_{1}+v_{3}+v_{5}=w_{1}+w_{3}+w_{5}=0\,.

We deform the middle
12
-gon until its shape approaches the swiss cross on the left of Figure
5
. In particular, as
w_{1}
becomes parallel to
w_{6}
(and thus
w_{4}
parallel to
w_{3}
) and at the same time
v_{5}
and
v_{6}
become parallel (and thus
v_{2}
and
v_{3}
), the small hexagons disappear and this swiss cross (or the regluing on the right) is actually the full top level and in the swiss cross these pairs of sides should be identified. The small crosses inside the swiss cross are the regular fixed points of the double cover involution. The gray point is the unique double zero visible on the top level. The black and white squares are the newly formed (regular) fixed points, which thus become the zeros at the top of the edges that connect to the lower level. The banana graph in
\Gamma_{\rm I}
(after smoothing the horizontal edge) is the unique level graph that is consistent with the aforementioned data.

Figure 5.
The top-level component of Type I and a regluing (sides with the same symbol
\alpha_{j}
being identified)

We can now compute the top-level volume contribution. Disregarding the marked points, the top level consists of the square-tiled surface
L_{3}
on the right of Figure
5
. Its Veech group is the theta group
\Gamma_{\theta}=\langle\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr),\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\rangle
which has index three in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Consider the action of the theta group on the five Weierstrass points, where the differential form is regular (i.e., has a “zero of order zero”). It is transitive and the image is a dihedral group
D_{5}
of order
10
. Since in our setting all the special points are marked, the top level is a degree-
10
cover of the Teichmüller curve generated by the
L_{3}
surface.
Since
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}(0)}\xi=-1/24
, we conclude that

(47)

\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma_{\rm I}}}\xi\;=\;10\cdot 3\cdot\frac{-1}{24}\;=\;\frac{-5}{4}\,.

In order to understand the sizes of the cylinders that are formed at the lower level, we look more closely at the degenerating hexagons. This lower level is given (in the neighborhood near the limit) by cutting out along the pinched curve, as shown in Figure
6
on the left. After redrawing, splitting each of the shaded regions in the middle, and taking these to the limit, the lower level (without yet having acquired a horizontal node) is given on the right of Figure
6
. The lower level acquires a cylinder (in fact a pair of cylinders, exchanged by the involution, hence
{\mathcal{M}}
-parallel cylinders), once the lower level is deformed so that
v\wedge y
changes sign.

Figure 6.
Left: A neighborhood (near the limit) of the lower level. (Small arcs around the points in the central
12
-gon are not drawn.) Right: The limiting lower level (in gray) after regluing. (The two planes should be extended to infinity to form poles.)

We now choose a basis according to the requirements of Lemma
3.4
. We take
\alpha_{1}=z+y
to be the core curve of the cylinder, and take
\beta_{1}=2z
to be a symplectically dual curve, normalized with some hindsight. We compute from Figure
4
and the equations in (
46
) that the total area of a surface in the gothic locus is

(48)

\mathrm{Area}=6v_{1}\wedge v_{2}+6w_{2}\wedge w_{3}+4v_{1}\wedge w_{2}-2v_{1}\wedge w_{3}-2v_{2}\wedge w_{2}+4v_{2}\wedge w_{3}\,,

where we use the same letter to denote both a path and its period. We now take

(49)

\alpha_{2}\;=\;-3v_{1}+3v_{2}+3w_{3}\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{2}\;=\;-v_{1}-v_{2}-2w_{2}-3w_{3}\,.

Since by construction
z=2w_{1}-w_{2}
and
y=2v_{2}-v_{1}
, a direct computation implies that

\mathrm{Area}=a_{1}\wedge b_{1}+a_{2}\wedge b_{2}

as requested by the normalization. We conclude, since the cylinders are simple and hence do not admit any fractional Dehn twist, that

(50)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{i}=1\,,\quad\mathfrak{b}_{i}=1/2\ ({\rm for}\ i=1,2)\,,\quad R=1\,,\quad\mathcal{L}=2\,,\quad c^{\operatorname{area}}_{\Gamma_{\rm I}}=2\,,

where the last identity follows since there is only one prong-matching and since the bottom level is uniquely determined by the topological type, forcing the map
\sigma
in Definition
3.9
to have degree one.

It remains to determine the factor
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm I}}}
. Observe that in the preceding top-level volume discussion, all the marked points, including the two upper half-edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
of the vertical edges, have been fixed. We choose a double zero to be on top, which gives
3
choices. For the remaining two double zeros, due to the bottom symmetry, we have a unique choice to label them. Once they are labeled, each individual vertical half-edge on the bottom is distinguished and connects to
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, respectively. It follows that this type has
3
components. For each component, the bottom of the graph admits an involution that swaps the two unmarked horizontal edges, while this involution maps the bottom differentials (up to simultaneously scaling)
[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
to
[-\omega_{1},-\omega_{2}]=[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
. Hence, it is a non-trivial automorphism of the concerned multi-scale differential and preserves the gothic locus. Therefore,
|{\rm Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm I}}}(\Gamma_{\rm I})|=2
.

In summary, the contribution of all components in Type I to the area Siegel–Veech constant is

c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm I})\;=\;3\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{-\frac{5}{4}}{\frac{13}{16}}\right)\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{15}{13}\,.

6.3.2.
Type II: The compact-type graph

From now on, we will be more brief by stating the cylinder geometry only rather than presenting the full degeneration and normalization discussion. This case has two cylinders exchanged by the involution. We choose
\alpha_{1}
as the core curve of one of them and
\beta_{1}
so that
a_{1}\wedge b_{1}
gives the cylinder’s volume contribution to the total area. Since the cylinders have modulus
1/2
, we find
\mathfrak{a}_{i}=1
and
\mathfrak{b}_{i}=1/2
for
i=1,2
. Since all zeros are marked, there is no fractional Dehn twist (i.e.,
R=1
) and we conclude that

c_{\Gamma_{\rm II}}^{\operatorname{area}}=2
.

Next, suppose the two top-level vertices are distinguished (i.e., their vertical edges are labeled). Then, we have
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma_{\rm II}}}\xi=-1/24
, due to the involution of each individual top vertex (but not swapping them).

To compute the factor
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm II}}}
, we choose a double zero to be on the right vertex of the bottom, which gives
3
choices of labeling. For each choice, the cardinality of the bottom level has been computed to be
c=2
in
[
Che22
, Section 2, Case (II)]
. It follows that this type has
6
components. For each component, there is an involution that swaps the two top vertices and simultaneously swaps the two bottom horizontal edges. This involution maps the top differential
[\omega,-\omega]
to
[-\omega,\omega]
and maps the bottom differentials
[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
to
[-\omega_{1},-\omega_{2}]
. Hence, it is an automorphism of order
2
of the multi-scale differential and preserves the gothic locus. Therefore,
|{\rm Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm II}}}(\Gamma_{\rm II})|=2
.

In summary, the contribution of all components in Type II to the area Siegel–Veech constant is

c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm II})\;=\;6\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{-\frac{1}{24}}{\frac{13}{16}}\right)\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{13}\,.

6.3.3.
Type III: The asymmetric cherry

In this case, there are two pairs of cylinders exchanged by the involution (as the graph
\Gamma_{\rm III}
shows), where one pair would be combined into a bigger cylinder if the zeros of order zero were unmarked. The flat geometric picture shows that the three combined cylinders have the same modulus, where the large one is twice as high and wide as the two smaller ones. Choosing
\alpha_{1}
to be the core curve of the long cylinder
and
\beta_{1}
according to the total volume normalization, we find

(51)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{i}=1\,,\,\,\mathfrak{b}_{i}=1/3\quad({\rm for}\ i=1,2)\,,\quad\mathfrak{a}_{i}=1/2\,,\,\,\mathfrak{b}_{i}=1/3\quad({\rm for}\ i=3,4)\,,

\displaystyle R=1\,,\qquad\mathcal{L}=3\,,\qquad c_{\Gamma_{\rm III}}^{\operatorname{area}}=3\cdot\Bigl(\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{3}+\frac{2}{3}+\frac{2}{3}\Bigr)=6\,.

To compute the degree of
\xi
on the top level, we break the vertical edges and label their upper ends. Then we have
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma_{\rm III}}}\xi=-1/4
, because the top level covers
\mathbb{P}\Omega\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}(0)
with degree
6
.

To compute the factor
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm III}}}
, we choose a double zero to be on the right vertex and choose an ordinary zero of order zero to be not on the bottom, which gives
3\cdot 3=9
choices. Once the double zeros are all labeled, the vertical edges are distinguished as well. It follows that this type has
9
components.
For each component, the bottom of the graph has an involution, which sends all bottom differentials to their opposites and which can act independently of the top-level involution. Therefore,
|{\rm Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm III}}}(\Gamma_{\rm III})|=2
.

In summary, the contribution of all components in Type III to the area Siegel–Veech constant is

c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm III})\;=\;9\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot 6\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{-\frac{1}{4}}{\frac{13}{16}}\right)\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{27}{13}\,.

6.3.4.
Type IV: The V-shaped graph

The length ratios of the cylinders in this case are the same as in Type (II) and we obtain again
c_{\Gamma_{\rm IV}}^{\operatorname{area}}=2
. To compute the top-level degree of
\xi
, we break the vertical edges and choose one of them to pair with the one of the two vertices on the top left that carries the same sign with the vertex on the top right. We thus conclude that
\int_{{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{Y}^{\top}_{\Gamma_{\rm IV}}}\xi=2\cdot(-1/4)=-1/2
.

To compute the factor
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm IV}}}
, choosing a double zero on the right vertex gives
3
choices. Hence, this type has
3
components.
For each component, the two top left vertices can swap, which induces an involution automorphism of the graph, while the self involution of the top right vertex has already been taken into account in the top degree of
\xi
. Therefore,
|{\rm Aut}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\Gamma_{\rm IV}}}(\Gamma_{\rm IV})|=2
.

In summary, the contribution of all components in Type IV to the area Siegel–Veech constant is

c_{\rm area}^{\rm int}(\Omega\mathcal{G},\Gamma_{\rm IV})\;=\;3\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{-\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{13}{16}}\right)\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\cdot\frac{6}{13}\,.
